1. Datasets and matching

Both runs cover the complete masternode list as discovered over SPV, and both contain exactly 2,605 records — no sampling on either side. This report therefore compares every node, not a subset.

 IllinoisSE Asia
records in dataset2,6052,605
reachable (status=done)2,174 (83.5%)2,171 (83.3%)
unreachable431434
mean score (reachable)97.494.0
median score (reachable)97.795.7
grade A / B / C / D2136 / 38 / 0 / 01838 / 308 / 14 / 11
uniform headers_target52,84852,611
highest advertised height2,502,8822,502,705

Matching is done on pro_tx_hash first, with address used only as a fallback for rows a hash cannot place. In practice the fallback was never needed: all 2,605 nodes matched by hash (0 required the address fallback), leaving 0 unmatched Illinois rows and 0 unmatched SE Asia rows. The two runs also agree on every node's address — 0 of 2,605 pairs disagree — so the masternode set did not churn measurably between them. The overlap is the entire population, which removes the sampling caveat that limited the earlier 50-node version of this report.

The workloads are closely comparable. Illinois explicitly used --sync-depth 50000 and produced a uniform 52,848-header target; SE Asia produced a uniform 52,611-header target consistent with the same setting. The 237-header difference (<0.5% of the workload) follows the later Illinois tip anchor. Median bytes served differ by ~1% between runs, confirming both vantage points asked for and received substantially the same data.

2. Reachability: near-total agreement

Across all 2,605 overlapping nodes the two vantage points agree on reachability for 2,602 (99.88%). 2,171 nodes were reachable from both, 431 were unreachable from both, and only 3 disagree — all in the same direction.

 SE Asia: reachableSE Asia: unreachable
Illinois: reachable2,1713
Illinois: unreachable0431

The 3 disagreements are nodes Illinois reached and SE Asia did not; there is no node in the opposite cell. Every one of the 431 mutually-unreachable nodes failed with the same "no connection within 15s" error from both observers — these are down or firewalled nodes, not observer artifacts. Reachability is overwhelmingly a property of the node population rather than of the observer, and the 83.5% / 83.3% reachable rates are effectively the same number.

reachable from Illinois onlypro_tx_hashkindIL scoreIL gradeIL connect msSE Asia result
132.243.197.49:99992bcf790720a5f494a6904e1341387c431a157db74fc925e9db3310e2a41b6c20Regular92.2A587no connection within 15s

Three nodes out of 2,605 is well inside the noise floor for two non-simultaneous runs: a node restarting, a peer slot filling up, or a transient route flap explains any of them. The result should be read as "reachability agreement is essentially total", not as evidence of a directional filtering effect.

3. Grades and scores: agreement is good, but drift is one-directional

2,171 nodes were graded from both vantage points. Exact letter-grade agreement holds for 1,845 of them (85.0%). Of the 326 disagreements, 312 move the node down when graded from SE Asia and only 14 move it up — the drift has a clear direction, not a random spread.

Illinois grade ↓ / SE Asia grade →ABCDFtotal
A1824287139—2,133
B142112—38
total18383081411—2,171

The dominant off-diagonal cell is A→B (287 nodes): a node that Illinois calls an A is often a B from SE Asia, purely because the extra round-trip latency costs it points. The tail is small but sharp — 9 nodes fall from A straight to D, and 13 from A to C.

Score correlation

Pearson r = 0.12 across 2,171 nodes (Spearman ρ = -0.17) — essentially no linear relationship. Because both distributions are tightly bunched near the top of the scale, correlation is a poor summary here: the scores agree closely in absolute terms while barely tracking each other in rank order. A node's ranking from one vantage point does not predict its ranking from another.

Score centres

Illinois median 97.7 vs SE Asia median 95.7 (means 97.4 vs 94.0). The same nodes score a few points lower when probed from farther away. Illinois never scores a node below 75.6; SE Asia bottoms out at 43.3.

Absolute difference

Mean 4.3 pts, median 2.4 pts. 1,635/2,171 (75.3%) stay within ±5 pts and 1,949 (89.8%) within ±10; only 31 nodes swing by more than 20 points.

Direction

Illinois scores the node higher for 1,886 of 2,171 nodes (86.9%); SE Asia scores it higher for 285 (13.1%). Mean signed difference (IL − SEA) is +3.4 pts, median +2.0.

Read together, these say something more precise than "grades disagree". For the great majority of nodes the two observers land within a few points of each other, so the score is a reasonably stable measurement of the node. What shifts is the whole distribution: SE Asia applies a consistent latency tax that pushes a large band of borderline A nodes across the B line, and occasionally — when a probe also hits a timeout — drops one much further.

4. Latency and throughput distributions

Across the 2,171 nodes measured by both runs, every latency metric is worse from SE Asia and every throughput metric is better from Illinois. The medians are not close.

metricvantagep25medianp75p95maxmedian ratio SEA/IL
connect time (ms)Illinois2222523055673,7091.71×
SE Asia3964255149105,443
avg ping (ms)Illinois2152542884452,9681.66×
SE Asia39041545876310,146
header throughput (hdr/s)Illinois6,0417,0827,9198,805105,7540.72×
SE Asia3,1574,8156,0368,012105,412
filter throughput (filt/s)Illinois10,29612,24513,54614,84116,2380.73×
SE Asia5,3008,34710,52713,64718,642
total probe time (s)Illinois10.311.313.027.0175.91.37×
SE Asia13.116.224.975.0180.2
bytes served (MB)Illinois25.926.026.226.328.20.99×
SE Asia25.625.926.126.335.7

The median node connects 1.71× slower and pings 1.66× slower from SE Asia, serves headers at 0.72× the Illinois rate and filters at 0.73×, and takes 1.37× as long to complete the same probe. Bytes served are near-identical (0.99×), which is the control: the same work was done, just more slowly. The tails are where the damage concentrates — 169 SE Asia probes exceeded 60 seconds (vs 15 from Illinois) and 34 exceeded 120 seconds (vs 2), pressing against the 180-second probe budget.

Timeouts follow the same pattern. 63 nodes recorded at least one timeout from SE Asia versus 38 from Illinois; 30 nodes timed out from both, 33 only from SE Asia, and 8 only from Illinois. No node in either run recorded a validation failure, so nothing here is a correctness problem — it is entirely a speed problem. Timeouts are also what produce the severe grade collapses: of the 25 nodes SE Asia graded C or D, 12 hit a timeout, and 22 of them were graded A from Illinois.

All nine of the worst collapses (A→D, roughly 98 → 45) share one signature: zero timeouts from Illinois, exactly one timeout from SE Asia. Eight of the nine also show a collapsed SE Asia header rate. The ninth, 93.190.140.162:9999, is the instructive case — its SE Asia header rate (37,264 hdr/s) is actually higher than its Illinois rate, and it still grades D, because the single timeout dominates the score on its own. These are not nodes that are quietly mediocre from Asia; they are nodes whose probe fell off a cliff once, and the grade follows. A D this far below the Illinois grade reflects one bad probe, not a consistently bad node.

The gains are milder than the drops (largest 16.4 pts vs 54.9) and split into two kinds. Some are genuinely Asia-proximate — 43.229.77.46:9999 connects in 37 ms from SE Asia versus 476 ms from Illinois — and some, like 151.244.85.8:9999, connect slower from SE Asia yet still score higher because their Illinois probe happened to serve headers slowly (1,053 hdr/s) on that particular run. The second group is a reminder that a single probe carries real per-run noise independent of geography.

6. Geographic interpretation

The latency gap is not uniform — it tracks where the node is hosted. Only 375 of 2,171 nodes (17.3%) connect faster from SE Asia, and only 292 (13.4%) ping faster; the remaining 1,796 connect faster from Illinois. That asymmetry is consistent with a Dash masternode population concentrated closer to Illinois than to the SE Asia observer, placing Illinois nearer the measured network's latency centre of mass.

Grouping the overlapping nodes by leading address octet (a rough proxy for hosting region — no geolocation database is used, so these are observed latencies, not asserted locations) makes the split explicit.

104.x33144518+41999.094.5

The 8.x block (135 nodes) is the cleanest SE Asia-proximate signal in the dataset: median connect 427 ms from Illinois versus 80 ms from SE Asia, and the median score flips from 95.7 to 99.7 accordingly. Blocks such as 162.x and 5.x mirror it in the other direction, connecting in tens to hundreds of milliseconds from Illinois and roughly half a second from SE Asia. Large blocks such as 188.x, 132.x, and 46.x sit between those extremes, which is why the aggregate favours Illinois so heavily.

Node type makes no difference at all, which is a useful negative result: the geographic effect is about the network path, not about what the node is running.

8. Data & sources

illinois-results.csv — controlled Illinois run, complete network (2,605 nodes) se-asia-results.csv — public SE Asia run, complete network (2,605 nodes) Original public Google Sheet (SE Asia run)

Illinois run configuration (logged): mainnet, --sync-depth 50000, filters enabled, concurrency 8, 180-second probe timeout, complete-network scope, chain tip 2,502,882. SE Asia run configuration: sync depth and timeout inferred from the data (uniform 52,611-header target, longest probe 180.2 s); concurrency is not recorded anywhere in its output.

9. Conclusion

Reachability is an absolute property of the node. Across the full 2,605-node network the two vantage points agree 99.88% of the time on whether a node answers at all, with 3 disagreements in total. Anyone asking "is this node up?" gets the same answer from Chicago or Singapore.

Performance grades are observer-relative. The same 2,171 nodes agree on letter grade 85.0% of the time, and where they disagree the node is downgraded from SE Asia 312 times out of 326. The median node connects 1.71× slower and serves headers at 0.72× the rate from SE Asia. The absolute score gap is modest for most nodes (median 2.4 pts) but it is systematic, and it is enough to push 287 nodes from A to B on its own.

A single-vantage-point health report therefore describes network health as seen from here. That is a legitimate and useful thing to measure — it is what a wallet in that region would actually experience — but it is not an absolute ranking of node quality, and it should not be published as one.

10. Limitations

  • The runs were not simultaneous. The Illinois run reports a chain tip of 2,502,882 and the SE Asia run 2,502,705 — roughly 177 blocks apart, so the two observers saw the network at different moments. Any node that restarted, changed provider, or hit transient congestion in between contributes a difference that has nothing to do with geography.
  • SE Asia concurrency is unknown. Its CSV does not record probing parallelism, so it cannot be compared with the Illinois run's concurrency of 8. If the SE Asia run probed more nodes in parallel, some of its lower throughput and longer probe times could be local resource contention at the observer rather than the network path — this alternative explanation cannot be ruled out from the data available, and it applies to the throughput figures more than to the connect/ping latencies.
  • Each region was probed exactly once. There are no repeated rounds, so per-run noise cannot be separated from a stable geographic effect. The largest outliers in section 5 are precisely where this matters: a single timeout can cost 50 points, and one probe cannot tell a persistently bad node from an unlucky one.
  • Sync depth is inferred for SE Asia. Every reachable SE Asia row shares a uniform headers_target of 52,611, consistent with a ~50,000-block depth, but the setting is not logged. Illinois explicitly used --sync-depth 50000.
  • Geography is inferred from address blocks and measured latency, not from a geolocation database. The regional groupings in section 6 are a reading of the latency data, not authoritative host locations.
  • Score correlation is a weak instrument here. Both score distributions are compressed near the top of the scale (Illinois: 2,136 of 2,174 reachable nodes graded A), so rank-order statistics like r = 0.12 overstate the disagreement. The distributional comparisons in section 4 are the more trustworthy evidence.
  • Timeouts are pass/fail per probe and dominate the scoring curve when they occur. Grades in the C–D range should be read as "this probe hit a timeout", not as a graded statement about sustained node quality.

11. Recommendations for future multi-vantage reports

  • Run every vantage point with an identical explicit configuration (sync depth, filters, concurrency, timeout) and log that configuration in every run's output — never leave it to be inferred, as it had to be here.
  • Run simultaneous, repeated rounds from each region rather than one-off passes, so transient conditions can be separated from persistent geographic effects and a single timeout stops being able to cost a node 50 points.
  • Aggregate per node across vantage points — reachability as a quorum, plus median and worst-case score — instead of trusting any single observer's grade.
  • Report score alongside the latency distribution it came from. The grade compresses connect time, ping, throughput and timeouts into one letter, and section 5 shows that letter can be driven entirely by one timeout.
  • Record region, timestamp, and full probe configuration alongside every report so cross-run comparisons like this one do not require reconstruction after the fact.
